       - organism that is not a cause of endocarditis would not support a diagnosis of

       - should be drawn. Lumbar puncture (LP) should be performed in nearly all patients
       - to obtain cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) for establishing diagnosis and determining
       - infectious organism/susceptibility. Adjust antimicrobial regimen to optimize
       - therapy and treatment duration based on pathogen once isolated.
       - Intravenous dexamethasone therapy improves outcomes for meningitis with
       - Streptococcus pneumoniae and is recommended for empirical treatment of all
       - community acquired cases that might be associated with S. pneumoniae.
       - Dexamethasone must be given before (1 to 20 minutes) with antimicrobials.
       + antimicrobials should be initiated as soon as possible after obtaining blood

       + minutes) or concurrent with antimicrobials.

=== prosthetic-valve-endocarditis-empirical-therapy [local-clinical, auto] Prosthetic valve endocarditis--empirical therapy
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU EC PV EMP THERAPY (matched by name)
     [both replace clinical]
       - Early surgical consultation is essential.
       - If a patient is hemodynamically stable and the diagnosis is uncertain,
       - antimicrobial therapy can safely be withheld until the diagnosis is clear and
       - blood culture results are available to guide therapy. In all cases, be sure
       - adequate numbers of blood cultures are obtained before antimicrobials are given.
       - Empirical Treatment
       - Vancomycin 15 mg/kg IV q12h ($) [R] <AND> gentamicin 1 mg/kg IV q8h ($)
       - [R,O] <AND> rifampin 300 mg PO/NG q8h ($) [DI,O]
       + Consult Infectious Diseases in all cases.

       - ($) [DI,O] for 6 weeks <AND> gentamicin 1 mg/kg IV q8h ($) [R,O] for initial 2
       - weeks

       - 2 weeks
       + Consult Infectious Diseases in all cases.

       + commonly present on wounds that are moist or covered. There is no need to
       + treat patients with systemic antimicrobials simply because a wound culture is
       + positive for a potential pathogen. Topical antimicrobials are not effective
       + in nearly all circumstances and can select for resistant organisms or lead to
       + other adverse effects. Systemic antimicrobial therapy may be indicated if
       + there are signs of infection.

=== covid-19-mpls-antimicrobial-guideline [local-clinical, partial] COVID-19 MPLS ANTIMICROBIAL GUIDELINE
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU ABX COVID-19 MPLS ANTIMICROBIAL GUIDELINE (matched by name)

       - INPATIENT COVID-19 ANTIMICROBIAL TREATMENT GUIDELINES
       - Refer to the Minneapolis VAHCS COVID-19 SharePoint site for additional info.
       - Minneapolis VAHCS COVID-19 SharePoint Site
       - ***COVID-19 therapy is a rapidly evolving field, including the widespread
       - dissemination of therapies with little or no evidence for benefit. Therapies and
       - dosages not specifically listed here or in the IDSA treatment guidelines
       - should be discussed with the infectious diseases service. ***
       - [Click here] IDSA Guidelines Treatment/Management Patients with COVID-19
       + COVID-19 PHARMACOLOGIC RECOMMENDATIONS
       + **If patient is severly symptomatic and showing rapid worsening-consider
       + Infectious Disease consultation**
       + Treatments shown below are based on available clinical data and drug available.
       + Recommendations may be updated based on changes in drug availability and
       + emerging clinical data.
     [one replace clinical dated]
       - HOSPITALIZED DUE TO COVID-19
       - Refer to COVID-19 Pharmacological Inpatient Treatment Guidelines to determine
       - whether monotherapy or combination therapy with remdesivir and dexamethasone is
       - appropriate. For patients on dexamethasone with rapidly increasing oxygen needs
       - and systemic inflammation, add a second immunomodulatory drug (see
       - Immunomodulatory Treatment below)
       - Minneapolis VAHCS COVID Pharmacologic Inpatient Treatment Guidelines
       - Remdesivir (IV):
       - Remdesivir 200mg IV once on day 1, then 100mg IV daily for 4 additional days
       - < AND >
       - Lab orders for monitoring remdesivir treatment (CMP, CBC with diff, INR)
       - Dexamethasone (PO/IV):
       + **FDA Approved Therapy: Remdesivir
       + See below for full prescribing information including WARNINGS and PRECAUTIONS
       + [Link] Remdesivir Package Insert (FDA approved Oct 2020)
       + Please Note:
       + Remdesivir can shorten COVID symptom duration but does not affect mortality. It
       + appears most effective for patients needing supplemental oxygen but not
       + receiving mechanical ventilation. It has little benefit for patients not
       + requiring oxygen. The default duration of therapy is 5 days and DOES NOT require
       + Infectious Disease approval, extending therapy is of minimal benefit and DOES
       + require approval from ID staff.
       + **If patient is severly symptomatic and showing rapid worsening-consider
       + Infectious Disease consultation**
       + Monitoring required for Remdesivir:
       + 1. Daily laboratory monitoring includes:
       + Comprehensive Metabolic Profile, CBC with Diff, and INR
       + 2. Consider discontinuing drug if ALT levels increase to greater than 10 times
       + upper limit of normal
       + 3. Discontinue drug if ALT elevation is accompanied by signs or symptoms of
       + liver inflammation
       + Relative contraindications:
       + Use not recommended unless potential benefit outweighs potential risk.
       + Note: Discuss with Infectious Disease patients with these contraindications:
       + 1. Hepatic impairment (pharmacokinetics and need for dose adjustment unclear)
       + 2. Renal impairment: not recommended in patients with eGFR less than 30ml/min
       + 3. Pregnancy (maternal and fetal risks unknown)
       + Remdesivir is FDA approved for treatment of COVID-19 in hospitalized patients:
       + 1. Requirement: PCR-confirmed COVID-19
       + Note: Consult Infectious Disease for COVID-19 negative patients, but
       + clinically very consistent with COVID-19
       + If above requirements are met, place order for Remdesivir:
       + Remdesivir 200mg IV once on day 1, then 100mg IV daily for 4 additional
       + days ($$$$$) [R,H]
       + Note: Consult Infectious Disease for approval to extend therapy beyond 5 days
       + (maximum 10 days total therapy)
       + ____________________________________________________________________________
       + **Beneficial therapies:
       + Dexamethasone COVID-19 Regimen (RECOVERY trial)
     [one replace clinical]
       - Immunomodulatory Treatment
       - MVAHCS recommends baricitinib over tocilizumab in patients on high flow nasal
       - cannula oxygen or non-invasive ventilated patients that can take PO or NG. For
       - patients who cannot take PO or NG, or for those who are mechanically ventilated,
       - we recommend tocilizumab. These treatments are to be added on to dexamethasone,
       - with or without remdesivir. Consult Infectious Diseases for more details.

=== isospora [local-clinical, fallback] ISOSPORA
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU ABX ISOSPORA (matched by name)
     [none replace clinical dated]
       - RECOMMENDATIONS NOT AVAILABLE
       - Recommendations for this condition are not available within CDSS. Please refer
       - to facility guidelines or other clinical resources for recommendations.
       + DIARRHEA WITH CYSTOISOSPORA BELLI (FORMALLY ISOSPORA BELLI)
       + C. belli is associated with diarrhea in persons with HIV/AIDS and only rarely in
       + immunocompetent persons.
       + Primary treatment
       + Hydration and electrolyte replacement
       + _______________________________________________________________________________
       + Antimicrobial treatment of persons without HIV/AIDS
       + Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole 1 tablet DS PO Q12H for 7 to 10 days ($)
       + [R,DI]
       + _______________________________________________________________________________
       + Antimicrobial treatment of persons with HIV/AIDS
       + Infectious Diseases involvement is essential. Initial treatment of acute
       + infection is followed by secondary prophylaxis until CD4 count is >200 for >6
       + months in response to ART without evidence of active C. belli.
       + Initial treatment
       + Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole 1 tablet DS q6H for 10 days ($) [R,DI]
       + Secondary prophylaxis
       + Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole 1 tablet DS PO q72h ($) [R,DI]
       + References:
       + Guidelines for Prevention and Treatment of Opportunistic Infections in HIV
       + Infected Adults and Adolescents. AIDSinfo (2015) 1-282
       + Sanford keyword: Cystoisospora belli
       + Shane et al, Clinical Infectious Diseases 2017, 65(12):45-80
       + Up-to-Date article: Management of Cystoisospora (Isospora) infections

=== liver-amebic-abscess [local-clinical, fallback] LIVER AMEBIC ABSCESS
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU ABX LIVER AMEBIC ABSCESS (matched by name)
     [none replace clinical dated shared:656-StCloud]
       - RECOMMENDATIONS NOT AVAILABLE
       - Recommendations for this condition are not available within CDSS. Please refer
       - to facility guidelines or other clinical resources for recommendations.
       + AMEBIC LIVER ABSCESS
       + Serological testing for amoebiasis should be obtained in all patients with
       + suspected amebic liver abscess. Multiple sets of anaerobic and aerobic blood
       + cultures should also be obtained prior to starting antibiotics to exclude
       + pyogenic liver abscess, which is a much more common cause of liver abscess.
       + Serologic testing can be negative in a small number of patients with acute
       + presentation (symptoms <2 weeks). Repeat serology is usually positive. If repeat
       + serological testing is negative for amoebiasis or patient does not respond to
       + treatment of amebic liver abscess, patient should have further diagnostic
       + testing completed to exclude pyogenic liver abscess.
       + Drainage of amebic liver abscess should be considered in patients with large
       + lesions at risk of rupture.
       + Due to complicated treatment and follow up, Infectious Diseases consultation
       + is recommended in all patients with liver abscess.
       + Treatment of patients with confirmed or suspected amebic liver abscess (obtain
       + blood cultures prior to starting in suspected cases)
       + Paromomycin is nonformulary and must be ordered by Infectious Diseases.
       + Metronidazole 750 mg PO q8h for 10 days ($) [DI] <FOLLOWED BY> paromomycin
       + 25-35 mg/kg/day PO q8h for 7 days ($) [M]
       + References:
       + Mandell chapter: Infections of the Liver and Biliary System
       + Sanford keyword: Amoebiasis, Entamoeba histolytica
       + Solomkin et al, Clin Infect Dis, 2010, 50:133-64
       + Up-to-Date article: Extraintestinal Entamoeba histolytica amoebiasis

=== sexually-transmitted-infection [local-clinical, partial] Sexually Transmitted Infections (STI) (Navigation)
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU ABX SEXUALLY TRANSMITTED INFECTION (matched by name)
     [both replace clinical]
       - consult to HIV clinic for further discussion and evaluation for PrEP.
       - [Consult] ID Pre-Exposure Prophylaxis HIV
       + consult to MRTC clinic for further discussion and evaluation for PrEP.
       + Pre-exposure prophylaxis (or PrEP) is a way for people who do not have
       + HIV but who are at high risk of getting HIV to prevent HIV infection by
       + taking a pill every day. When someone is exposed to HIV through sex or
       + injection drug use, these medicines can work to keep the virus from
       + establishing a permanent infection.
       + When taken daily, PrEP is highly effective for preventing HIV. Studies
       + have shown that PrEP reduces the risk of getting HIV from sex by about
       + 99% when taken daily. PrEP is much less effective if not taken consistently.
       + As PrEP only protects against HIV, condoms are important for the protection
       + against other STIs. Condoms are also an important prevention strategy
       + if PrEP is not taken consistently.

=== mastoiditis [local-clinical, fallback] Mastoiditis
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU MASTOIDITIS (matched by name)
     [both replace clinical]
       - otitis media. Diagnosis is supported by CT or MRI imaging and culture of
       - drainage. Antibiotics and drainage of middle ear are sufficient treatment in
       - most cases. Mastoidectomy is necessary if abscess has formed in the mastoid
       - bone. If complication of first episode of acute otitis media, etiologies
       - typically include Streptococcus pneumonia (most common), Streptococcus pyogenes,

       - Chronic Mastoiditis
       - Chronic mastoiditis is usually the result of chronic otitis media with
       - perforation of the tympanic membrane. There is often associated formation of a
       - cholesteatoma (keratoma). Diagnosis is supported by CT or MRI imaging and
       - culture of drainage. Long term treatment with topical antimicrobials is first
       - line therapy. Infection is often polymicrobial, including Staphylococcus aureus,
       - Pseudomonas aeruginosa and anaerobes. Treatment should be managed in
       - collaboration with otolaryngology and includes topical antimicrobials, care of
       - external auditory canal, and is some cases mastoidectomy. If possible, withhold
       - therapy until a pathogen is identified by culture of drainage to direct therapy.
       - Specific drug choice dictated by culture results.
       - Empirical Treatment
       - Preferred:
       - Ciprofloxacin-Dexamethasone otic 4 drops in affected ear(s) BID for 4 weeks ($)
       + Ciprofloxacin-Dexamethasone otic 4 drops in affected ear(s) BID for 2 weeks ($)

       - [Click here] Minneapolis VAMC Isolation Precautions
       + Standard and Transmission Based Isolation
       + Standard (white): Wear Personal Protective Equipment (PPE)
       + for all patients when you anticipate exposure to blood and
       + body fluids.
       + Contact (green): Use for patients with c diff diarrhea/herpes
       + zoster with drainage/colonized or active infection of MRSA/
       + ESBL or multi drug resistant bacteria.
       + Strict/Special (red): Use for patient colonized or infected with VRE or VRSA.
       + Airborne Infection (pink): Use for patients with active or rule out
       + TB disseminated herpes zoster/measles/chicken pox or small pox.
       + Droplet Precautions (yellow): Use for patients with influenza
       + pertussis/diptheria/epiglottitis/meningococcal disease or
       + respiratory systemic Haemophillis Influenza.
       + Protective Isolation (blue): Use for any immunicompromised
       + patient/hand hygiene prior to entering room and do not care
       + for this patient if you are sick.

=== flu-seasonal-influenza [local-clinical, auto] Influenza vaccination recommendations
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU ABX INFLUENZA VACCINATION (matched by name)
     [both replace clinical]
       - Influenza Vaccine Outpatient and Inpatient Guidelines
       - Annual influenza vaccination is recommended for all persons aged 6 months and
       - older without a specific contraindication.
       - Eggs and latex allergies are NOT considered contraindications to receiving the
       - vaccine, the only contraindication to the flu vaccination is severe allergic
       - reaction (i.e. Anaphylactic reactions).
       - Recommend observing all patients for 15 minutes after receiving any influenza
       - vaccine. A previous severe allergic reaction to influenza vaccine, regardless of
       - the component suspected of being responsible for the reaction, is a
       - contraindication to future receipt of ANY influenza vaccine.
       - Outpatient (excluding ED) Influenza Vaccination Recommendations
       - #For age 65 or older administer Fluad Adjuvanted Trivalent 0.5ml syringe.
       - #For age 64 or under administer Afluria Trivalent 0.5ml syringe.
       + Annual influenza vaccination is recommended for all persons aged 6
       + months and older without a specific contraindication.
       + Eggs and latex allergies are NOT considered contraindications to receiving
       + the vaccine, the only contraindication to the flu vaccination is severe
       + allergic reaction (i.e. Anaphylactic reactions).
       + Recommend observing all patients for 15 minutes after receipt of any
       + influenza vaccine. A previous severe allergic reaction to influenza vaccine,
       + regardless of the component suspected of being responsible for the reaction,
       + is a contraindication to future receipt of ANY influenza vaccine.
       + Influenza Vaccination Recommendations:
       + Outpatient Vaccination:
       + >For age 65 or older administer Fluzone HIGH DOSE Trivalent 0.5ml syringe.
       + >For age 64 or under administer Flurix Trivalent 0.5ml syringe.
     [both delete clinical shared:636-Omaha,656-StCloud]
       - For those who do not trigger clinical reminder, order below:
       - Influenza once dose for outpatients that do not trigger clinical reminder
     [both replace clinical]
       - Inpatient and ED Vaccination Recommendations
       - Influenza orders are part of the admission order set. For those who require an
       - order due to admission order being discontinued/expired or if patient was
       - admitted prior to influenza season, use the order below:
       - [Click Here] Influenza Vaccination Inpatient and ED 25/26
       - #For age 65 or older administer Fluad Adjuvanted Trivalent 0.5ml syringe.
       - #For age 64 or under administer Afluria Trivalent 0.5ml syringe.
       + Inpatient and ED Vaccination:
       + Influenza is a protocolized immunization and does not require a medication
       + order. It is driven by a clinical reminder. Nursing will utilize the
       + "Immunization" Note in CPRS that links to all clinical reminders associated
       + with immunizations. This note allows for correct charting of the vaccine,
       + lot# and expiration date.
       + >For age 65 or older administer Fluzone HIGH DOSE Trivalent 0.5ml syringe.
       + >For age 64 or under administer Flurix Trivalent 0.5ml syringe.

=== rabies-pre-exposure-prophylaxis [local-clinical, partial] Rabies pre-exposure prophylaxis
  -- Inpatient: ORZID3 CI GMENU ABX RABIES PRE-EXPOSURE PROPHYLAXIS (matched by name)
     [both replace shared:656-StCloud]
       - For patients exposed to a rabid animal, refer to the Rabies Post-Exposure
       + For patients exposed to a rabid animal, refer to the Rabies-Post Exposure
     [both replace clinical shared:656-StCloud]
       - People in Risk Category Groups 1-4 (see below for full descriptions) should be
       - vaccinated against rabies.
       - Rabies vaccine, 2-dose series (0, 7 days)
       + People who are in high-risk groups should be vaccinated against rabies.
       + Rabies high risk groups
       + Rabies vaccine, 2 dose series
     [both replace clinical shared:656-StCloud]
       - A booster vaccination is given for groups 1 through 3 (not 4 or 5). For groups
       - 1 and 2, this is dictated by rabies serum titer levels. For group 3, a one-time
       - booster is recommended at a specific time interval. For those in risk category
       - 1, check titer every 6 months. For those in risk category 2, check titer every
       - 2 years). See below for information regarding timing of the one-time booster
       - for risk category 3.
       + People with continuous risk of rabies exposure should be tested for rabies virus
       + neutralizing antibody every six months. Those with frequent risk of rabies
       + exposure should be tested every two years.
       + Rabies risk categories
       + A booster vaccination should be administered if the serum titer falls to
       + maintain a value of at least complete virus neutralization at a 1:5 serum
       + dilution by the rapid fluorescent focus inhibition test.
     [both delete clinical shared:656-StCloud]
       - CDC guidelines categorize patients into five categories based on exposure risk
       - Risk Category 1 (Highest risk): People who work with live or concentrated rabies
       - virus in laboratories
       - Recommendation: Give rabies vaccination. Check titer every 6 months and give
       - booster dose if titer is low
       - Risk Category 2: People who frequently do at least one of the following: handle
       - bats, have contact with bats, enter high-density bat environments like caves, or
       - perform animal necropsies
       - Recommendation: Give rabies vaccination. Check titer every 2 years and give
       - booster dose if titer is low
       - Risk Category 3: People who interact with, or are at higher risk to interact,
       - with mammals other than bats that could be rabid, for a period longer than three
       - years after they receive PrEP. This group includes:
       - Most veterinarians, veterinary technicians, animal control officers, wildlife
       - biologists, rehabilitators, trappers, and spelunkers (cave explorers). Certain
       - travelers to regions outside of the United States where rabies in dogs is
       - commonly found.
       - Recommendation: Give rabies vaccination. Either a one-time titer check after 1
       - and up to 3 years following the first 2-dose vaccination < OR > 1-dose booster
       - between 3 weeks and 3 years following the first vaccine in the 2-dose
       - vaccination
       - Risk Category 4: Same population as risk category 3, but at a higher risk for ?
       - three years after they receive PrEP.
       - Recommendation: Give rabies vaccination. Routine boosters not recommended.
       - Risk Category 5:
       - People who do not have special occupational risks.
       - Recommendation: Rabies vaccination and boosters not recommended.
     [one replace clinical dated shared:656-StCloud]
       - Rao et. Al., MMWR. Morbidity and mortality weekly report 71 (2022)
       - https://www.cdc.gov/rabies/hcp/clinical-care/pre-exposure-prophylaxis.html
       + Briggs et. al., MMWR Morb Mortal Wkly Rep, 2022, 71:619-627
       + Manning SE, Rupprecht CE, Fishbein D, et al. Human rabies prevention-United
       + States, 2008: recommendations of the Advisory Committee on Immunization
       + Practices. MMWR Recomm Rep 2008, 57:1.
       + Rupprecht, Charles E., et al. Use of a reduced (4-dose) vaccine schedule for
       + postexposure prophylaxis to prevent human rabies: recommendations of the
       + advisory committee on immunization practices. MMWR Recomm Rep 59.RR-2 (2010): 1-
       + 9
  -- Outpatient: ORZID3 CI GMENU ABX RABIES PRE-EXPOSURE PROPHYLAXIS (matched by name)

=== pneumocystis-jirovecii-prophylaxis [local-clinical, partial] PNEUMOCYSTIS JIROVECII PROPHYLAXIS (PCP Prophylaxis)
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU ABX PNEUMOCYSTIS JIROVECII PROPHYLAXIS (matched by name)
     [one replace shared:636-Omaha,656-StCloud]
       - PNEUMOCYSTIS JIROVECII PROPHYLAXIS (PCP Prophylaxis)
       + PNEUMOCYSTIS JIROVECII PROPHYLAXIS
     [both replace clinical shared:636-Omaha]
       - for 3 to 6 months in patients, except that prophylaxis should be continued for
       - life in those who have had P. jirovecii disease.
       - Recommended FIRST-LINE prophylaxis:
       - Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole 1 DS tablet q day ($) [R,DI]
       + for 3 months in patients, except that prophylaxis should be continued for life
       + in those who have had P. jirovecii disease.
       + Preferred prophylaxis
       + Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole 1 DS tablet QD (Cost $) [R,DI]
     [both replace clinical shared:636-Omaha]
       - Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole 1 SS tablet q day ($) [R,DI]
       - Alternative when trimethoprim/sulfa is poorly tolerated or contraindicated:
       - **Patient should be tested for glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6-PD)
       - deficiency before starting dapsone. Individuals deficient in G6-PD
       - show about a 2-fold increase in sensitivity toward dapsone-induced hemolytic
       - anemia.
       - G6-PD lab test are resulted the same day or next day. May be add on lab.
       - G6-PD lab result should be "normal" to start dapsone therapy.
       - [Click here] G6-PD Screen Lab Order
       - Dapsone 100mg PO q day ($) [DI]
       - Alternative to the above preferred regimens:
       - Atovaquone is alternative for patient for clinical reason:
       - (e.g. patients with SLE, hematologic concerns, G6PD deficiency)
       - Atovaquone 1500mg q day for PCP prophylaxis ($$) [DI]
       + Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole 1 SS tablet QD (Cost $) [R,DI]
       + Alternative
       + Dapsone 100mg PO QD (Cost $) [O]
       + < OR >
       + Pentamidine 300mg Inhalation QMONTH (Cost $) [DI,O] <AND> Albuterol 0.083%
       + inhalation QMONTH (Cost $) [M] clinic orders (call 612-467-5193 for 3V LPN
       + appointment)
     [both delete clinical shared:636-Omaha]

=== prevention-of-prosthetic-joint-infection-with-dirty-operation-site [local-clinical, partial] Prevention of prosthetic joint infection before infected skin, skin structures or muskuloskeletal tissue procedures
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU ABX DERM IE JI PROPHYLAXIS (matched by name)
     [one replace clinical shared:636-Omaha]
       - PREVENTION OF PROSTHETIC JOINT INFECTION BEFORE INFECTED SKIN, SKIN STRUCTURES
       - OR MUSKULOSKELETAL TISSUE PROCEDURES
       - There is a lack of evidence supporting antimicrobial prophylaxis to prevent
       - prosthetic joint infections in patients undergoing procedures on infected skin,
       - skin structures or musculoskeletal tissue procedures.
       - For more information contact Infectious Diseases.
       + INFECTED SKIN SURGICAL SITE PROSTHETIC JOINT INFECTION PROPHYLAXIS
       + Antimicrobial prophylaxis is indicated in procedures involving infected skin
       + skin structures, or infected musculoskeletal tissue when the patient is at
       + high risk of hematogenous prosthetic joint infection.
       + Hematogenous prosthetic joint infection high risk conditions:
       + 1. First two years following joint replacement
       + 2. Previous prosthetic joint infection
       + 3. Immunocompromised
       + 4. Insulin dependent diabetes
       + 5. HIV infection
       + 6. Malignancy
       + 7. Malnutrition
       + 8. Hemophilia
       + If patient has one or more of the above conditions, antimicrobial prophylaxis is
       + indicated. Use the following link for antimicrobial prophylaxis recommendations.
       + Antimicrobial Prophylaxis in Patients with Prosthetic Joints
     [one replace clinical dated shared:636-Omaha,656-StCloud]

       - hardware infection

       + Rosengren et al, Am J Clin Dermatol (2010) 11.1, 35-44
       + Up-to-Date article: Antimicrobial prophylaxis for prevention of surgical site
       + infection in adults

       + endocarditis
       + Up-to-Date article: Skin surgery: Prevention and treatment of complications

=== hidradenitis-suppurativa [local-clinical, fallback] Hidradenitis suppurativa (Navigation)
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU HIDRADENITIS SUPPURATIVA (matched by name)
     [one replace clinical shared:636-Omaha,656-StCloud]
       - A chronic inflammatory condition that typically involves occlusion, rupture,
       - abscess formation and infection of hair follicles as well as secondary infection
       - of apocrine glands/surrounding tissues. Lesions most often occur in the
       - intertriginous areas (axillae, inguinal and anogenital) in is associated with
       - smoking and obesity. There is a 40% genetic predisposition for hidradenitis
       - suppurativa (HS) and is often accompanied by pain, malodor, and drainage.
       - Cultures are negative 50% of the time and often are polymicrobial with S.
       - epidermidis and S. aureus most isolated. The Hurley Staging System is often used
       - to determine severity of HS.
       - Therapy generally consists of treatment of comorbidities that can exacerbate HS,
       - avoidance of triggers, pain management, wound care, and topical/oral
       - antimicrobials. Hormonal agents, steroids, retinoids, and surgery can also be
       - used to treat certain patients. Biologics may be considered in Stage II-III HS.
       - Consult Dermatology in all cases
       - Hurley Staging System:
       - Stage I: recurrent nodules and abscesses with minimal scarring
       - Stage II: limited number of sinuses and/or scarring within a body region
       - Stage III: multiple or extensive sinuses and/or scarring
       - Duration of Therapy:
       - Treatment is typically for 12 weeks but may require longer durations in certain
       - cases. Metronidazole should only be used for a maximum of 6 weeks to avoid
       - neurologic toxicity.
       - Interventions for All Patients:
       - Smoking cessation, weight loss, avoidance of triggers, and pain management
       - should be part of all treatment plans. Consult Dermatology for use of hormonal
       - agents, steroids, retinoids, and surgery.
       - Optional Topical Treatment, May be Used in Addition to Oral Therapies Below:
       - Chlorhexidine gluconate 4% liquid topically wash affected area q24h ($) [M]
       - < OR >
       - Benzoyl peroxide 10% soap topically wash affected area q24h ($) [M]
       - < ADD ON THERAPY FOR FLARES - MUST USE IN ADDITION TO ONE OF THE AGENTS ABOVE>
       - Clindamycin 1% solution topically to affected areas q12h prn for flares ($) [M]
       - Stage I-II Initial Treatment:
       - Doxycycline 100 mg PO q12h ($) [DI] for 12 weeks
       - < OR >
       - Minocycline 100 mg PO q12h ($) [DI] for 12 weeks
       - Stage III Initial Treatment and Stage I-II Failure of Oral Doxycycline:
       - Clindamycin 300 mg PO q12h for 12 weeks ($) [H,O]
       - Stage II-III Failure of Oral Clindamycin:
       - Moxifloxacin 400 mg PO q24h ($)[R,DI] for 12 weeks <AND> metronidazole 500
       - mg q8h ($) [DI] for 6 weeks <AND> rifampin 300 mg PO q12h ($) [DI,O] for 12
       - weeks
       - _______________________________________________________________________________
       + Hidradenitis suppurativa results from occlusion of hair follicles and the
       + typical lesion is a pea-sized cyst. Symptoms include pruritus, burning,
       + erythema, and local hyperhidrosis.
       + Treat with topical antimicrobials. For severe disease, or if topical
       + antimicrobials are not effective, consult dermatology. Surgical excision may
       + be necessary for extensive disease.
       + Treatment
       + Treat for 3 months
       + Clindamycin 1% topical solution apply twice daily ($) [M]
     [none replace clinical shared:636-Omaha,656-StCloud]
       - Alikhan, Ali, et al. Journal of the American Academy of Dermatology 81.1 (2019):
       - 76-90
       - John Hopkins ABX Guide Keyword: Hidradenitis Suppurativa
       - Up-To-Date article: Hidradenitis suppurativa: Management
       - Up-To-Date article: Hidradenitis suppurativa: Pathogenesis, clinical features,
       - and diagnosis
       + Up-to-date website

=== tinea-unguium-onychomycosis [local-clinical, auto] Tinea unguium (onychomycosis)
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU TINEA UNGUIUM (ONYCHOMYCOSIS) (matched by name)
     [both delete clinical]
       - Topical Treatment:
       - Ciclopirox is a topical first-line treatment for both fingernails and toenails.
       - Complete resolution occurs in approximately 7 percent of patients treated daily
       - for 48 weeks. Ciclopirox is a well-tolerated treatment. Potential side effects
       - include temporary nail changes and local skin irritation.
       - Ciclopirox 8% nail lacquer: Apply once daily to affected nail(s)
       - Remove with rubbing alcohol every 7 days. [M] See accompanying medication
       - pamphlet for specific instructions. Treatment is continued until nail clearance
       - or up to 48 weeks.

=== cied-surgery-post-op-prophylaxis [local-clinical, partial] Cardiac Implantable Electronic Device (CIED) Surgery:
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU SURG CIED POSTOP (matched by name)
     [one replace clinical shared:656-StCloud]
       - Cardiac Implantable Electronic Device (CIED) Surgery:
       - *** Routine post-procedure antibiotics are not indicated. ***
       + Cardiac Implantable Electronic Device (CIED) Post-Op Prophylaxis Antibiotics
       + Post-procedure CIED IV antibiotic prophylaxis:
       + Place post-op IV antimicrobial prophylaxis after admission.
       + If new CIED implant, then give same antibiotic(s) as preop for 2 doses post-op
       + If generator replacement only, then no additional doses recommended.
       + Preferred POST-PROCEDURE prophylaxis:
       + For patients weight less than 120 kg:
       + Cefazolin 2 GM q8h for 2 doses post-op ($) [R]
       + For patients weight equal to or greater than 120kg:
       + Cefazolin 3 GM q8h for 2 doses post-op ($) [R]
       + Alternative in patients with beta lactam allergy:
       + Vancomycin 15mg/kg for 2 doses post-op ($) [R]
       + MRSA Positive Patients Post-Procedure antibiotics:
       + For patients weight less than 120 kg:
       + Cefazolin 2 GM q8h x 2 doses post-op ($) [R] < AND >
       + Vancomycin 15mg/kg IV q12h x 2 doses post-op ($) [R]
       + For patients weight equal to or greater than 120 kg:
       + Cefazolin 3 GM q8h x 2 doses post-op ($) [R] < AND >
       + Vancomycin 15mg/kg IV q12h x 2 doses post-op ($) [R]
       + __________________________________________________________
       + Post-procedure CIED ORAL TAKE HOME MEDICATIONS:
       + Preferred POST-PROCEDURE prophylaxis:
       + Cephalexin 500MG PO bid for 3 days AFTER CIED PROCEDURE
       + Alternative for patients with beta lactam allergy:
       + Clindamycin 300MG po qid for 3 days AFTER CIED PROCEDURE
       + MRSA Positive Patients Post-Procedure antibiotics:
       + Doxycycline 100MG PO bid for 3 days AFTER CIED PROCEDURE
     [one delete clinical shared:656-StCloud]

=== ophthalmic-surgery-prophylaxis [local-clinical, partial] Ophthalmic surgery prophylaxis
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU SURG OPHTHALMIC SURG (matched by name)
     [one insert]
       + HELP, additional information:
       + Help page (instructions, microbiology, drug properties, etc.)
       + Consult Infectious Diseases
       + Ophthalmology consult
     [one delete clinical]
       - Cataract Surgery
       - Intracameral moxifloxacin is recommended for use to reduce the risk of
       - post-operative endophthalmitis after cataract surgery only in those patients
       - at highest risk of infection, including the following:
       - * Patients with prior intravitreal injections
       - * Immunosuppressed patients
       - * Patients with significant ocular surface eyelid disease
       - * Complication occurs during surgery (i.e. capusle rupture, vitreous loss)
       - * Other possible high risk situations as they arise but not listed above
       - Preferred:
       - Moxifloxacin 500mcg/0.5ml Eye Inj Intracameral Cataract (Conc.=100mcg/0.1ml)
       - References:
       - Arshinoff S., J Cataract Refract Surg, (2016) 42:1730-1741

=== aspergillus-infections-general [local-clinical, fallback] Aspergillus species
  -- Inpatient: ORZID2 CI GMENU FUNGI ASPERGILLUS SPP. (matched by name)
     [both replace clinical shared:636-Omaha,656-StCloud]
       - Amphotericin B Liposomal 3-5MG/KG IV q24h ($$$$)[R,O]+ NS Hydratio
       + Liposomal amphotericin (Ambisome) 3-5 mg/kg IV q24h ($$$$) [R,O]
  -- Outpatient: ORZID3 CI GMENU FUNGI ASPERGILLUS SPP. (matched by name)
     [none replace clinical shared:636-Omaha,656-StCloud]
       - -Aspergillus species are ubiquitous, but invasive aspergillosis occurs primarily
       - in immunocompromised hosts. Neutropenia and glucocorticoid use are the most
       - common predisposing factors. Optimal management involves early diagnosis and
       - early initiation of antifungal treatment.
       - -The most common clinical manifestation of invasive aspergillosis is pulmonary
       - disease, but other manifestations include central nervous system infection,
       - rhinosinusitis, endocarditis, gastrointestinal disease, annd others.
       - Duration of antimicrobial therapy:
       - Duration of therapy should be a minimum of 6 to 12 weeks, although duration
       - is highly dependent on degree/duration of immunosuppression, disease site, and
       - and evidence of disease improvement. Consult Infectious Disease.
       - Severe disease:
       - Refer to Inpatient CDSS for treatment.
       - Mild/moderate disease:
       - Preferred treatment:
       - Voriconazole 400 mg PO BID for 2 doses, then 200 mg PO BID ($$) [R,H,DI]
       - Non-VA order for above
       - Alternative treatment:
       - Consult Infectious Disease for alternative.
       - References:
       - Patterson TF, et al. Clin Infect Dis. (2016) 63(4):433-442
       - UpToDate: Treatment and prevention of invasive aspergillosis
       + Refer to Inpatient Antimicrobial CDSS.
